Figure 7: Technological development phases. Correlation between accumulated production volume (MWh) and price.

PTES

Crucial knowledge regarding PTES in the context of Danish district heating systems has been gathered
through the last decade. Thus, PTES is categorized as “early category 3” or TRL9 on IEAs TRL scale as the key
development potentials lie in increased standardization in work procedures as well as the development of high
temperature resistant liners at feasible prices, which is to be seen as improvement of existing designs.

BTES

The one demonstration plant in Denmark (Brædstrup – pilot project) is evaluated positively as a technical
solution since implementation and operation has worked as expected. However, no full-scale storage is yet
implemented in Danish district heating systems. Further development of BTES is expected through means of
standardization of processes and improved modelling of the energy system, resulting in optimized storages
for the given system.

ATES

ATES is a well-proven technology in e.g. Sweden and The Netherlands and Denmark, primarily for cooling of
individual commercial buildings and alike. In the context of Danish district heating, it is still a niche technology.
However, as many key assumptions and knowledge about the technology can be adjusted to the Danish


                                           47

PAGE 48
140 Seasonal Heat Storage



context, ATES is assumed to be in development category 3 or TRL9 on IEAs TRL scale, as there is yet only
limited locally embedded expertise in combination with district heating, but the experiences from the building
sector and industries can easily be adopted by the district heating sector. For The Netherlands TRL is 10-11
for shallow ATES for buildings.

Uncertainty
The above is based on available information regarding the development of technical solutions in PTES, BTES
and ATES. Hence the uncertainty as to the state-of-the-art data is very low. However, the results are sensible to
the stated circumstances, system integration etc. I.e. the data can only be applied to the stated limitations as
to e.g. soil conditions (soil material, absence of flowing groundwater etc.).

Additional remarks
Under Danish climatic conditions long term storage for solar heat typically requires 0.3-2 m3 water storage
volume per m2 solar collector, with solar heat contributing 20-40 % of the total yearly heat load (cf. technology
element Solar district heating in this Technology Data Catalogue). For systems with higher solar fraction, the
marginal additional storage volume needs to be about 4 m3 per m2 solar collector [5 & 9].

141 Tank Thermal Energy Storage

Qualitative description

Contact information
Danish Energy Agency: Andrei David Korberg (ankrg@ens.dk), Christoph Wolter (chwo@ens.dk)

Authors:
Romain Sucche, rsu@planenergi.dk, PlanEnergi
Geoffroy Gauthier, gg@planenergi.dk, PlanEnergi

Publication date
November 2018

Amendments after publication date
 Date         Description
 July 2026   Update of the technology description and datasheet including the addition of
            50.000m3 TTES

Brief technology description
Introduction to thermal storage
Thermal storage options can be split into three different technologies: [1] [2]
     1.  Sensible heat storage, which uses the heat capacity of the storage material – mainly water for its high
        specific heat content per volume, low cost and non-toxic medium.
     2.  Latent heat storage, which makes use of the storage material’s latent heat during a solid/liquid phase
       change at a constant temperature.
     3.  Thermochemical storage, which stores heat through reversible chemical processes. This includes
        sorption technologies, in which thermal energy is exchanged through adsorption or absorption
       between material pairs such as zeolite–water (adsorption) and water–lithium bromide (absorption).


The market is dominated by sensible Tank Thermal Energy Storages (TTES) due to the quality, the cost, the
simplicity and the versatility of water as a storage medium. A Tank Thermal Energy Storage is a sensible heat
storage and may be constructed as steel, concrete or glass-fiber reinforced plastic tanks (depending on the
temperature levels of the water inside). For higher temperature applications (such as district heating), the
most common type of structure is a steel tank. Latent heat and thermochemical storage technologies are not
the topic of this chapter and are therefore not described further.

TTES description
In the context of this catalogue, TTES are above-ground, insulated steel tank structures, installed on a concrete
foundation. They are connected to a district heating network and supplied with one or several5 inlet diffusers in
the upper part of the tank, and an outlet diffuser at the bottom for charging. The cycle is reversed for
discharging (see Figure 1). A tank may be supplied with more than two diffusers, to increase the flexible use of
the thermal stratification inside the storage (hot water at the top, colder water at the bottom and intermediate
temperatures in-between).





5 The use of multiple diffusers makes it possible to charge and discharge TTES at different temperature levels (e.g., 97°C
and 80°C)



                                           51

PAGE 52
141



This chapter focuses on atmospheric tanks (vented to the atmosphere via an inert gas cushion) and also
mentions pressurized tanks, without going into details.

Figure 1: Illustration of the charging and discharging process of a TTES [3]





 Figure 2: Main stages of on-site TTES construction [3]      Figure 3: Picture of the 2*1,000 m3 TTES in Præstø (DK), total
                                                                                                    height of 19.3m [4]





According to the definition provided by IEA ES Task 39, TTES systems are large‑scale sensible thermal energy
storage systems. They are designed to store a large amount of heat (at least 1 gigawatt‑hour of heat6 per





6 1 gigawatt‑hour of heat corresponds roughly to the annual heat demand of about 75 households, based on an average
consumption of 13.4 MWh/year for a Danish household, as reported by the Danish Energy Agency [17]



                                           52

PAGE 53
141



year7), at temperatures above 50°C such that the stored heat can be delivered to district heating networks,
while operating at atmospheric pressure. [3] This definition suits well the type of thermal storages encountered
in Danish district heating systems.

Application in Danish District Heating Systems
From the 1980s through the 1990s, Denmark expanded its decentralized Combined Heat and Power (CHP)
system by building new CHP plants and converting older plants to natural-gas-based CHP. To manage the
mismatch between heat production and heat demand, these plants widely adopted TTES. TTES allowed plants
to shift heat consumption and production, enabling them to generate electricity during the most profitable
hours. Early tanks were sized according to the 3-tier electricity tariff system and typically stored enough heat
to cover 2–3 days of summer demand. [5]
Around 2005, the introduction of variable spot-market tariffs enabled natural gas–fired CHP plants to operate
more flexibly, running mainly when electricity prices were high. With this new market flexibility, CHP plants
could be combined with solar thermal plants in the same district heating system, and TTES could benefit both
the CHP and solar thermal plants. During the summer period, solar thermal plants supplied most of the heat,
since solar energy availability is high and heat demand is lower, while CHP plants could reduce operation or
run only when electricity prices were favorable. TTES also provided winter peak shaving and operational
reserve, which made them attractive for biomass-based plants as well. Tank sizes were then often determined
by how many winter hours the storage could supply. Up until the end of 2019, solar thermal plants could
provide tax credits in the heat savings credit scheme, which boosted the development of solar district heating
(and corresponding means of thermal storage, both seasonal heat storage technologies like Pit Thermal
Energy Storage “PTES” and smaller scale heat storage like TTES).

Figure 4: Principal scheme of the hydraulic integration of a TTES in a simple district heating system [6]





From 2019 onward, improved economics for electric heat pumps shifted investment away from solar thermal.
Large-scale heat pumps increasingly became the preferred new heat source.
By 2025, over 180 Danish district heating utilities had installed heat pumps with a total thermal capacity of
about 600 MW, complemented by roughly 1,500 MW of electric boilers. Some of these units provide
grid-balancing services, with TTES now functioning as key flexibility assets for power-to-heat operations.
A 2013 survey by the Danish District Heating Association reported that 284 district heating plants8 were
equipped with TTES. Together, these TTES provided a total storage capacity of 34 GWh and a combined


7 Note that this value refers to annual throughput, and not a storage capacity. In practice, relatively modest storage
volumes (e.g. a 5,000 m³ tank with around 0.3 GWh of usable capacity) can easily exceed 1 GWh/year when cycled
multiple times

8 80% of the respondents



                                           53

PAGE 54
141



volume of 711,000 m³. The average TTES size was approximately 2,500 m³, with most installations falling
within the typical range of 500-5,000 m3. [7] Today, continued investment in new TTES – driven by revenue
opportunities in the electricity market – has doubled this capacity. Nowadays, the cumulated TTES capacity9
almost reaches 69 GWh, corresponding to a total TTES volume of about 1,400,000 m³. [8]

Figure 5: Accumulated volume of TTES (m³) and capacity (MWh) in district heating - 442 TTES considered [9]





In summary, TTES have been widely adopted in Denmark over the past 40-50 years. This stands in contrast to
most other European countries. In Germany, for example, such tanks are typically installed in very large district
heating systems, usually as a single, larger unit, also operating at higher temperatures (up to about 100°C),
while in France the technology is still relatively new and not widely deployed. As a result, Denmark has gained
far more practical and technical experience with this type of system than other countries. This higher level of
experience also influences TTES prices: the cost figures provided below apply to Denmark only and should not
be considered representative for other markets.

Energy efficiency
TTES are a well-established technology. Typically, TTES for daily use in district heating applications are
insulated with about 300 mm insulation (of e.g. mineral wool), but for long-term storage, 400 mm may be
more suitable. [8]

The size and height-over-diameter ratio influence heat losses. A theoretical calculation of three different sizes
(500, 1,000 and 5,000 m3) with a height/diameter ratio of 1.8 shows heat losses of 2.1 %, 1.7 % and 1.0 % per
week at 90°C water temperature and 0°C outside temperature, 10 m/s wind and 300 mm insulation. [7]

To increase energy efficiency and usability of thermal storage, it is often chosen to directly connect TTES to
the district heating system rather than installing a heat exchanger between them. This is feasible provided that





9 In 2026, the total storage capacity installed in Denmark - combining both TTES and PTES systems - reached
approximately 100 GWh, corresponding to a total storage volume of about 2,000,000 m³.



                                           54

PAGE 55
141



the pressure level is compatible and that the water quality in the district heating network meets the TTES’
water quality requirements (see next section).

Input/output
Hot water, max. approx. 97°C10.

If the tank is pressurized or designed as a 2-zone tank, higher temperature can be achieved – for example in
the range of 100-120°C. [10] [11] However, such designs remain relatively uncommon in Denmark, are usually
limited to large-scale installations, and involve significantly higher specific costs compared to non-pressurized
TTES. Note that these tank types are not considered as part of the scope of the quantitative analysis
presented below.

The water used in TTES must meet specific quality requirements to minimize corrosion and prevent premature
damage. In particular, the water should have a pH of 9.8 ± 0.2 at 25°C and contain only very low
concentrations of particles and components such as oxygen, iron, chlorides etc.11

Typical input/output heating capacity
The input and output heating capacity of TTES can span a wide range. While this parameter is not inherent to
the TTES themselves and is primarily determined by the hydraulic design (specifically the sizing of the pumps
and diffuser pipes installed), it is not entirely unconstrained. At higher capacity levels, the design must still
ensure that a stable thermocline can be maintained and that the diffuser system can distribute flows without
excessive mixing. Consequently, although the project owner can specify charging and discharging capacities
based on technical and economic considerations, these choices must remain compatible with sound thermal
behavior and feasible diffuser design.

The figure below illustrates typical capacity ranges observed in practice for TTES: approximately 0.5–50 MWₜₕ
for a 1,000 m³ tank, and around 5–1,000 MWₜₕ for a 50,000 m³ tank.





10 Assuming TTES are equipped with a nitrogen‑based inerting system, which is the most commonly used solution in
Denmark. With a steam‑based system, however, the storage can operate at higher temperatures, typically in the range of
100–105°C

11 A slight overpressure at the top of the tank - maintained by an inerting station using either steam or a nitrogen cushion -
prevents oxygen from entering the tank and helps reducing the risk of internal corrosion



                                           55

PAGE 56
141




Figure 7: Overview of the range of heating power available12 from the different types of large thermal energy storage
systems [3]





Typical capacities
Typical capacities vary with each district heating plant. Sizes of 500-10,000 m3 are very common in a Danish
district heating context. Over time, the trend has shifted towards larger TTES. TTES commissioned between
1975 and 2012 have an average volume of approximately 2,200 m³, whereas installations commissioned from
2013 onward are larger, with average volumes approaching 5,000 m³ [9].

Figure 6: TTES for district heating commissioned in Denmark since 1975, ordered by storage volume (m³) [9]





12 These graphs are estimates based on the knowledge of IEA ES Task 39 experts, not the result of a technical study. For
PTES and ATES, several single pits or pairs of wells are assumed to be used to achieve higher total water eq. volumes



                                           56

PAGE 57
141



As shown in Figure 7, very large-scale installations are also emerging, such as in Aalborg and Aarhus. In
Aalborg, the utility has recently commissioned four 50,000 m³ TTES, while in Aarhus, two more are planned.
For this reason, the datasheet on the Quantitative Description focuses on two representative cases: a 5,000 m³
and a 50,000 m³ TTES. It should also be noted that most TTES are either below 20,000 m3, or above 40,000
m3. This can be explained by the fact that above 20,000 m3, the scale of the tanks makes them more difficult
to handle during construction, requiring more advanced construction methods, more robust foundations, etc.
This has an impact on the TTES construction costs. Danish TTES manufacturers have so far focused on
construction of TTES below13 20,000 m3, which means that for larger TTES projects, manufacturers from
abroad are hired, also contributing to higher costs (since local suppliers and workforce are usually not
employed).

The storage capacity of TTES in terms of stored energy depends on the temperature difference and therefore
also the temperature levels. The change of production technologies towards e.g. heat pumps and solar
thermal results in lower temperatures (e.g. 70-80°C), whereas the temperature is higher (e.g. 90-100°C) if the
heat is produced by a CHP-plant or a boiler. Hence, the capacity of the TTES in terms of energy is likely to
change, depending on the heat production technology. It should also be noted that in practice, only
approximately 90 % of the storage capacity can be used. [7]

The table below summarizes the typical capacities for different TTES volumes14.

  TTES volume    m3   500  1,000  2,500  5,000  10,000  50,000

 Typical capacity MWh  29    58    144   288    576    2,878



The formula for calculating the thermal capacity of TTES is the following:

                                Q = ρ · V · c · ΔT

Considering that:

    •  Q = m · c · ΔT
    •  m = ρ · V

Where:

    •  Q = heat content (J)
    •  ρ = density of water (kg/m³)
    •  V = volume of water (m³)
    •   c = specific heat capacity of water (≈ 4180 J/kg·°C)
    •  ΔT = temperature difference (°C)





13 For volumes between 20,000 m3 and 40,000 m3, Danish district heating utilities will likely build several smaller tanks
(provided they have enough space available), like for instance in Silkeborg, where 4*16,000 m3 TTES have been built, see
https://www.silkeborgforsyning.dk/fjernvarme/produktionsanlæg-og-brændselssammensætning/solvarmeanlæg

14 Considering 55K of temperature difference in the tank and 90 % availability



                                           57

PAGE 58
141



Typical Storage Period
The typical storage period depends on the heat demand and typically varies from a few hours to approx. two
weeks. Additionally, TTES can be used for covering peak demands, i.e. to cover morning and evening peaks by
being charged during the night and throughout the day.

In smaller district energy systems, TTES can be used for seasonal storage, when the desired storage capacity
is too small to require e.g. a pit thermal energy storage (cf. Seasonal Heat Storage, chapter 140). For storages
up to approx. 25,000 m3 storage volume, TTES have generally proven to be more cost-effective than e.g. small-
scale pit thermal energy storages. [4]

Regulation ability and other system services
TTES enhance system flexibility by decoupling heat production from demand, allowing operators to optimize
generation based on electricity market conditions.

    •   In combination with CHP units, it enables shifting heat production to periods of high electricity prices,
        while stored heat covers demand during low-price periods.
    •  When integrated with power-to-heat technologies such as heat pumps or electric boilers, TTES can
        also contribute to electricity grid balancing by providing ancillary services, thereby creating additional
       revenue streams while supporting efficient and low-cost heat supply.

Space Requirements
The typical height‑to‑diameter ratio of TTES varies with its volume and is largely influenced by municipal limits
on maximum allowable height - often capped at around 30 m to reduce visual impact. For smaller TTES15, the
height‑to‑diameter ratio16 generally ranges from 1.0 to 2.5, while for larger TTES17 it typically falls between 0.7
and 1.2 (see Table 1). TTES of intermediate size (10-20,000 m3) also have intermediate ranges of
height‑to‑diameter ratio (0.8 to 1.5).

The footprints of TTES with 350 mm insulation & cladding, 55K temperature difference, 40 m2 for piping and
service area and 90 % availability (for thermal storage capacity calculations) are estimated in the table below.
These footprints indicate the minimum area required to implement TTES, but in practice, more space is often
required to move around the tank, have access to buried pipes, the technical building, etc. and TTES end up
using more space (and potentially several times the footprint) than what is indicated in the table. For the
footprint of very-large scale TTES, it is assumed that a square around the tank is necessary (instead of a
circle), and a service area of 400 m2.





15 Volume below 10,000 m³

16 Note that a H/D ratio of 1 also provides the smallest heat losses for a given volume

17 Volume above 20,000 m³

Advantages/disadvantages
Advantages

    •   Well-known technology
    •   Increases short-term flexibility of operation in district heating plants
    •   Enables a higher share of renewable heat at relatively low cost
    •  Can in some cases keep the pressure in district heating systems
    •   Cost-effective storage of heat
         o  The most cost-effective storage medium for thermal energy storage at low (0 - 20°C) to
            medium (20 - 100°C) temperature is water, because it is relatively cheap, environmentally
                  friendly and convenient medium. Furthermore, water has, compared to other common
               storage materials, a very high specific heat capacity as well as a very high volumetric heat
                capacity and possibility of temperature stratification.
    •  Low investment cost
    •   Supports sector coupling by integrating heat production with the electricity system
         o  CHP units can adjust their production according to electricity market prices, generating heat
            when electricity prices are high and relying on thermal storage when prices are low
         o  TTES can help provide grid services when combined with a heat pump or an electric boiler,
               generating additional revenue on top of producing low‑cost heat for later use
    •   Flexible and adaptable output power range [3]


Disadvantages

    •  Space requirements
    •   Energy losses18
    •  A nitrogen or steam cushion is required in atmospheric TTES to prevent oxygen ingress and thereby
        protect the system against corrosion


Environment
Large TTES may have an influence on the surrounding landscape. However, as they are typically installed next
to district heating plants, this influence is assessed to only have limited impact.

The risk of leakage of treated water is a possible environmental threat. However, major leakages happen very
seldom.





18 Although rather limited (typically less than 5% per year for a daily storage) [4]



                                           59

PAGE 60
141



Research and development perspectives
The research and development of TTES in Danish district heating systems is assessed to be limited to
adjusted operation strategies of the existing technological solutions. This includes:

    •   Operation at lower temperatures and temperature differences in district heating grids, resulting in
        lower energy content per water volume.
    •  Use of TTES for cooling storage.
    •   Using one TTES for storage at different temperature levels to accommodate the optimal supply
       temperatures for heating and cooling purposes, as well as the different temperature levels of separate
       heat sources (such as a large-scale heat pump, solar thermal or a boiler).

Additional secondary minor research and development topics may include:
    •  Advancements in more efficient and cost‑effective welding and assembling techniques (e.g., spiral
       assembling technologies similar to those used by Rodoverken Group AB)
    •   Opportunities for co‑utilization of thermal storage (e.g., integration with PV systems, commercial
          facilities such as restaurants on the top of TTES, etc.) or development of more diverse and
         aesthetically appealing cladding solutions
    •   Approaches for retrofitting existing installations (e.g. old oil tanks)
    •   Underground / partially underground TTES
    •   Sector coupling and new business opportunities for TTES (e.g. grid services)


Examples of Market Standard Technology
TTES were already installed in more than 280 district heating systems as of 2013 and are thus widely applied.
Below are several recent or upcoming projects in Denmark that illustrate continued development in recent
years: [7]

    •   5,000 m³ TTES for Haderslev Fjernvarme, built by Steel Tank A/S in 2023
    •   4,200 m³ TTES for Bjæverskov Fjernvarme, built by FW Rørteknik A/S in 2025
    •  4 × 50,000 m³ TTES for Aalborg Forsyning, built by Rodoverken Group AB in 2025 [12]
    •   12,000 m³ TTES for Fjerritslev Fjernvarme, built by FW Rørteknik A/S in 2026
    •  2 × 50,000 m³ TTES planned for Aarhus in the coming years [13] [14]

Prediction of performance and costs
TTES are a mature and proven technology; hence, the technology is in category 4 “Commercial”. The
development potential comprises storage at different temperature levels.

Figure 8: Technological development phases. Correlation between accumulated production volume (MW) and price

Quantitative description
Case of a 5,000 m3 TTES

The scope considered for the quantitative description of the 5,000 m3 TTES case includes:

    •  Tank & equipment: Delivery of the assembled steel tank (typically according to EN 14015), diffusers,
        corrosion protection, insulation/cladding, nitrogen system, instrumentation, valves19 and other
        TTES‑related interface components.
    •    Civil works: Site access, earthworks, and foundations.
    •   Consultancy: Project management and supervision.

The scope is limited to the tank itself and the required foundations and design studies. It excludes any external
components such as a potential technical or hydraulic building, pumps, heat exchangers, pipes and valves -
except those welded/attached directly to the tank - as well as the pipes connecting the TTES system to the
district heating network. The cost of connecting TTES to district heating networks can vary significantly
depending on each project and the distance to the existing network. In Denmark, TTES are typically placed in
peri‑urban areas close to central heating facilities, which usually keeps connection costs at a reasonable level.

Note that the quantitative description is limited to atmospheric TTES; therefore, pressurized systems or tanks
designed as two-zone configurations are excluded from the scope here.





19 Those integrated directly into the tank



                                           61

PAGE 62
141



Economy of scale
TTES installations benefit strongly from economies of scale, which can be reflected in a power‑law regression
trend, illustrated in the figure below. The scope underlying this cost function is described in the previous
section [4].

Figure 9: Specific CAPEX formula for TTES in Denmark [4]





The formula for the power‑law regression trend, only valid for TTES volumes ranging from 900 m³ to
12,000 m³, can be expressed as follows:

                                     P = a·V^b

Where:

    •  P = Specific CAPEX (EUR/m³)
    •  V = TTES volume (m³)
    •   a = 3,055 (-)
    •  b = -0.309 (-)

Case of a 50,000 m3 TTES

In Europe, including Denmark, there has been a growing tendency to build increasingly large TTES systems for
major district heating networks. Recent examples include the four 50,000 m³ TTES built for Aalborg Forsyning
by Rodoverken Group AB in 2025. Aalborg Forsyning has chosen to invest in the four TTES because the tanks,
together with the electric boilers, the seawater heat pump, surplus heat from Aalborg Portland and waste heat
from Nordværk, will help replace the coal-based district heating from Nordjyllandsværket when it closes down
in 2029. Two 50,000 m³ TTES are also planned in Aarhus in the coming years [12] [13] [14] [15]. In Germany, a
52,000 m³ TTES facility in Berlin was commissioned in 2023 to support the city’s district heating network,
connected to a 200 MW power‑to‑heat plant that utilizes surplus wind power to provide renewable heat for
approximately 13 hours. [16]

The scope considered for the quantitative description of the 50,000 m3 TTES case includes:

    •  Tank & equipment: Delivery of the assembled steel tank (typically according to EN 14015), diffusers,
        corrosion protection, insulation/cladding, nitrogen system, instrumentation, valves and other
        TTES‑related interface components.



                                           62

PAGE 63
141



    •    Civil works: Site access, earthworks, and foundations.
    •   Connection works: All of the piping (including civil work), required valves, dedicated pumps, heat
        exchangers, small technical building, control system, and connection to the existing district heating
        network.
    •   Consultancy: Project management and supervision.

Note that the quantitative description is limited to atmospheric TTES; therefore, pressurized systems or tanks
designed as two-zone configurations are excluded from the scope here.

Higher cost & uncertainty
The scope of work associated with very large TTES (volumes around and above 40,000 m3) is wider than the
one of smaller TTES, since installing such large TTES requires more integration to the existing system
(connection to the district heating network, variety of heat sources, size and control of the pumps).

At such large scales, the steel plates thickness and the height of the tanks require specific and more costly
construction methods (for welding, lifting, and foundations building), that are not used by Danish tank
manufacturers. The lack of references of TTES at these large scales and limited number of international
suppliers able to build such large TTES in Denmark also increase the specific price of such thermal storages.
This explains why the specific costs of very large TTES are higher than those of smaller TTES20.

The numbers provided in this datasheet should also be used with caution, since they are based on only a few
references, and could vary significantly from case to case, amongst other things depending on the required
connection works (distance to the district heating network, controls, technical building, etc.).

142 Household Hot Water Tanks

Qualitative description

Contact information
Danish Energy Agency: Andrei David Korberg (ankrg@ens.dk), Christoph Wolter (chwo@ens.dk)

Authors
Romain Sucche, rsu@planenergi.dk, PlanEnergi
Elsabet Nomonde Noma Nielsen, elsa@dtu.dk, DTU
Simon Furbo, sifu@dtu.dk, DTU
Geoffroy Gauthier, gg@planenergi.dk, PlanEnergi

Publication date
May 2012

Amendments after publication date
 Date            Description
 July 2026      Update of descriptions and datasheets
 August 2019    Major update of text and data


Brief technology description
A household hot water tank is an insulated tank that stores and maintains a supply of heated water for
domestic uses such as showers, taps, and appliances. Cold water enters the tank from the mains, is heated by
a source like an electric element, boiler, or heat pump, and kept at a set temperature until needed. When a tap
is opened, hot water is delivered from the tank while fresh cold-water refills it, ensuring a continuous supply.

For solar domestic hot water, the heat exchanger from the solar collectors is usually placed in the bottom of
the hot water tank (cf. the lower coil in Figure 1). Often, an extra coil is placed in the top of the tank to raise the
temperature by an additional heat source, when needed. For shift load storage, there is no need to have heat
exchanger coils, if for example the storage is a component in a closed circuit with a heat pump.





                                           65

PAGE 66
142 Household Hot Water Tanks




                         Figure 1: Typical domestic hot water storage used for solar heating [1]





Purpose
Hot water storage tanks in private homes are used for different purposes:

    •  To increase the operating periods for e.g. heat pumps, biomass boilers and solar heating systems and
       hence facilitate more efficient operation of these technologies.
    •  To enable load shifting by storing21 energy during periods of low-cost, off-peak electricity and using it
          later, thereby transferring part of the peak demand to off-peak periods. Reshaping the load curve
       improves the utility's capacity factor and, by extension, its financial performance.
    •  To ensure sufficient flow for high domestic hot water demands and space heating

Construction and materials
Hot water tanks are typically made of steel that is corrosion-protected by enamel and a sacrificial anode.
Equipment such as heat exchanger spirals, electric heating elements, stratification devices for enhancement
of thermal stratification in the hot water tanks, baffle plates for reducing mixing by incoming water during
discharge and charge, etc., can be built into the tanks. The hot water tanks are normally insulated with an
insulation material with a low thermal conductivity to reduce heat losses of the tanks. Often polyurethane
(PUR) foam is used as insulation material.

Normally, the tank material, as well as all the equipment inside the tank, is heated to the same temperature as
the water in the hot water tank.

Key thermal characteristics
The performance of a hot water tank depends on several factors:





21 For storages heated by heat pumps or electric heating elements



                                           66

PAGE 67
142 Household Hot Water Tanks



    •   Heat storage capacity

The heat content of the hot water store in a specific temperature interval, from Tmin to Tmax is determined by
the product of the heat storage capacity and the temperature difference (Tmax - Tmin).

More precisely, the heat content of a hot water tank in a temperature interval from T1 to T2 is determined by
the following formula:

                Q = (T2  - T1) x [(Mwater x Cwater) + (Msteel x Csteel) + (Mhtf x Chtf)]

Where:

       -   Q [J] is the heat content of a hot water tank
       -     Mwater (kg): mass of the water in the tank
       -     Cwater (J/kg·K): specific heat capacity of water
       -     Msteel (kg): mass of the tank’s steel
       -      Csteel (J/kg·K): specific heat capacity of steel
       -     Mhtf (kg): mass of the heat transfer fluid in the tank
       -     Chtf (J/kg·K): specific heat capacity of the heat transfer fluid

In standard hot water tanks, the water accounts for nearly all the total heat content of the storage volume.

Achieving a compact hot‑water tank requires storing as much heat as possible within the smallest volume.
This depends not only on the water volume but also on the tank’s shape and on how the insulation is designed
and applied.

    •   Heat exchange capacity rates to and from the hot water tank

The heat exchange capacity during charging should be high enough to ensure that the efficiency of the energy
system supplying heat to the tank is not significantly reduced. If the heat transfer fluid must reach excessively
high temperatures to deliver heat, system performance will decline.

Similarly, during discharging, the heat exchange capacity should be sufficiently high to allow the tank to deliver
the required heat output at an adequate temperature whenever needed.

    •   Temperature stratification in the hot water tank

During operation, thermal stratification will develop within the hot‑water tank, meaning that the upper region
becomes significantly warmer while the lower region remains at a lower temperature.

The efficiency for most energy systems with hot water tanks can be increased if a large thermal stratification
is built up both during charge and discharge periods for the hot water tank. Furthermore, thermal stratification
should be maintained to a great extent in periods without charge and discharge of the hot water tank.

For instance, a highly stratified hot water tank for a solar heating system ensures that the solar collector
temperature during operation stays low and that the solar collector performance therefore becomes high, and
that the requirement for auxiliary energy supply is reduced to a low level due to high temperatures of the hot
water drawn from the top of the hot water tank.

Heat losses mechanisms and mitigation

    •   Heat losses definition



                                           67

PAGE 68
142 Household Hot Water Tanks



The heat loss from the hot‑water tank should be kept as low as possible to ensure that the overall
performance of the energy system is not significantly reduced.

The heat loss of a hot water tank is determined by the product of the heat loss coefficient of the tank and the
difference between the hot water tank temperature and the ambient temperature. The heat loss coefficient is
theoretically determined by traditional heat transfer theory by means of the thermal conductivity and thickness
of the insulation material and of the shape of the tank/the surface area of the tank. That is, the theoretically
calculated heat loss coefficient represents the heat transmission through the insulation material.

As a reference, today's EU energy-labelled household water heaters (class A or B) reach standby losses
corresponding to approx. 0.5-1.0 W/K at similar sizes, achieved with thicker PUR insulation (typically 5-8 cm).

    •   Thermal bridges

Beyond the normal conductive heat loss through the insulation, considerable additional losses occur via
thermal bridges. These thermal bridges may result from inadequate insulation installation, localized reductions
in insulation thickness, or penetrations of any kind through the insulation. Heat loss is also influenced by
external components attached to the tank, as these can create further thermal bridges.

Measurements have shown that the position of thermal bridges strongly influences the magnitude of heat
loss. A thermal bridge at the bottom of the tank cools the adjacent water, which settles and forms a stable,
insulating cold layer, thereby minimizing heat loss. However, when a thermal bridge is located near the top of
the tank, the cooled water is displaced by warmer, lighter water rising from below. This continuous convective
replacement keeps the thermal bridge at a high temperature, resulting in substantially increased heat loss. [2]

    •   Piping

The piping connected to a hot‑water tank plays a major role in overall heat loss. A pipe penetration through the
insulation forms a thermal bridge and can cause substantial heat loss because temperature‑dependent
density differences may induce internal fluid circulation within the pipe. This circulation transfers heat into
portions of the piping system, increasing the tank’s heat loss. To avoid this, pipe connections should be
located at the bottom of the tank and routed downward, preventing natural circulation in the piping.

Pipe connections located on the top or side of a hot‑water tank lead to considerable heat loss. This can be
mitigated by installing a heat trap on the pipe just outside the tank. The heat trap is constructed as a
downward‑oriented pipe section near the tank outlet, connected to a horizontal section, which in turn leads to
an upward‑oriented segment. Figure 2 provides a schematic comparison of a top‑mounted pipe connection
with and without a heat trap.





                                           68

PAGE 69
142 Household Hot Water Tanks




Figure 2: Sketch of a tank with a pipe connected to the top without and with a heat trap





Moreover, when a pipe connected to the hot‑water tank forms part of a loop, thermosyphon effects may
develop within the circuit, causing substantial heat losses. It is therefore essential to install a valve that
remains closed during periods without flow to suppress unintended natural circulation.

To summarize, preserving thermal stratification requires that the upper region of the tank is fully insulated and
free of thermal bridges. All components that may act as thermal bridges - such as piping connections or
structural attachments - should be located at the bottom of the tank. Additionally, pipe loops passing through
the tank must contain a valve to prevent thermosyphon effects.

Typical capacities
Small domestic hot water tanks for single-family houses and apartments typically have a volume between 60
and 300 liters, depending primarily on the heat source and the hot water demand. Hot water tanks up to 300
liters are often pre-insulated and designed to fit inside 60 cm × 60 cm × 200 cm cabinets commonly used in
utility rooms.

Typical Storage Period
The typical storage period is a few hours, facilitating appropriate operation of the heat production capacity,
with close-to-constant operation of the heat source, at varying heat loads.

Advantages/disadvantages
Advantages:

    •   Small hot water tanks can be used as storage to facilitate shift load storage to capture the cheaper,
        off-peak electricity and use it at other times, effectively shifting portions of peak load to off-peak
        hours22
    •   Decentralized production units may be operated more efficiently when combined with storage for an
         efficient and stable supply of domestic hot water and space heating
    •  Cheap and easy to produce (millions of 50-1,000 L tanks produced internationally each year)
    •   Well proven technology





22 Reshaping the load curve improves the utility's capacity factor, which in turn improves the economic performance of the
system.



                                           69

PAGE 70
142 Household Hot Water Tanks



Disadvantages:

    •   Comparably large footprint, partly due to use of steel and insulation material
    •   Depending on domestic hot water tank temperatures, legionella bacteria inside the tank may be an
        issue
    •  May cause high return temperature in district heating systems, which results in higher energy losses.
        Furthermore, the possibilities for lowering temperatures are weakened in traditional systems, during
       summertime, and generally in low temperature district heating systems, due to the higher flow
       temperatures required compared to heat exchanger substations

Environment
There is no local environmental impact from household hot water tanks.

Research and development objectives
Improvements of thermal storages using water as storage medium are primarily within the following areas: [3]

    •   Development of optimally designed mantle tanks, which can establish and maintain thermal
         stratification in a much better way than traditional hot water tanks with built in heat exchanger spirals
    •   Hot water tanks with built in inlet stratifiers used to enhance thermal stratification
    •   Smart storage operation through flexible storage temperatures in (parts of) the storage tanks

Examples of Market Standard Technology
Small-scale water tanks are installed in most buildings with biomass boilers, heat pumps and/or solar thermal
and many buildings with other heating sources.

Prediction of performance and costs
Figure 3: Technological development phases. Correlation between accumulated production volume (MW) and price





Traditional water tanks are in category 4 “Commercial”, i.e. it is mature technology and there is only a limited
development potential.

143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage


143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage

Contact information
Danish Energy Agency: Emil Rasmussen, emrm@ens.dk
Author: Morten Herget Christensen mohc@teknologisk.dk

Publication date
February 2025

Amendments after publication date
 Date       Description
 Feb 25   New chapter and datasheet


Qualitative description

Brief technology description
Carnot batteries are systems that convert electricity into heat, store the heat in thermal energy storages, and
convert it back into electricity. This is shown in simplified form in Figure .

The family of technologies covered under the term Carnot batteries is quite broad. In this description, we focus
on the sub-group of Carnot batteries that store heat in sensible heat and convert it back into electricity using a
Rankine cycle.

Figure 1 Typical Carnot battery (Vandersickel 2023)

                                       power                                                            Thermal                                                                                                                                                       power                                                                                                                                                                                                             Thermal
                                           Thermal
                                             Energy
    Electrical                  Thermal        Storage     Thermal                        Electrical                  P2T                                              T2P    power                   power                   power                   power

               Conversion                                             Conversion
              from electrical                                       from thermal
                to thermal                                                  to electrical




In the following sections the focus of the technology description is on Carnot batteries with three main
properties:

    •   Input conversion using resistive electrical heating.
    •   Sensible heat storage using molten salt or hot rocks to store at > 200 °C.
    •   Output conversion using steam generators and Rankine cycle for power generation.

We also consider use cases where the steam is not used for the regeneration of electricity but is used for
industrial processes. Strictly speaking, these use cases are not Carnot batteries, but they are relevant for
certain business cases related to thermal energy storage.

PAGE 73
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



This focus reflects the status of the Danish commercial solutions available for Carnot batteries at the time of
writing and thus is not meant to exclude other potential designs that could be included in future updates as the
technologies mature.

Applications of the technology
The main use case for the type of Carnot batteries that is the focus of the chapter has been chosen to reflect
the status of the most advanced pilots and demonstration plants in Denmark in autumn 2024. These are
centered on replacing gas consumption for industrial applications by electrification of steam production. In
such cases industrial natural gas boilers are replaced or supplemented by Carnot batteries that produce
process steam or heat for industrial processes using electricity as input, as shown in Figure 2. The Carnot
battery is designed to store amounts of energy, typically covering anywhere from a few hours up to two weeks
of energy demand. The input power of the Carnot battery can often be designed independently of the storage
and production capacity, in order to take advantage of local conditions, i.e. maximizing self-consumption of
behind the meter renewable electricity or price arbitrage in electricity markets.

Figure 2 Replacement or supplementation of natural gas boilers by Carnot batteries





For industrial steam production the temperature requirements of the process become an important factor
when considering the type of Carnot battery, as the temperature used in the thermal energy storage must be
higher than the process requirements. For industrial processes both rock-based and molten salt-based Carnot
batteries can be used.

A similar use case for Carnot batteries is for the replacement or supplementation of coal or biomass in
combined heat and power production as shown in Figure 3 where the steam production from the Carnot
battery is used to drive a steam turbine for electricity production and heat or steam is extracted or condensed
for process or district heating use. Designs have been shown that can cover the thermal needs of centralized
coal- or biomass-fired power plants (>400MW) for a storage duration up to two weeks. However, the first real-
world implementations outside of concentrated solar power applications still remain to be realized.

PAGE 74
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage




Figure 3 Replacement or supplementation of coal or biomass by Carnot batteries





For combined Heat and power applications with steam cycles, the storage temperature needs to be very high.
The molten salt Carnot batteries have been shown with temperatures approaching 600 °C, while rock-based
storages from Danish manufacturers have significantly lower storage temperatures. For some rock-based
storage designs the heat transfer fluids limit the maximum temperature in the storage. Other designs use gas
(typically air) as the heat transfer medium, allowing for rock-based storages to be operated at much higher
temperatures suitable for integration in CHP plants, however for such designs it can be challenging to reach
high output power, due to lower heat capacity of gases.

Input conversion
In a Carnot battery, the main input is electricity that is converted to heat. This is typically done using resistive
electric heating, this technology is commercially available for common heat transfer fluids, in a wide range of
power capacities and temperature ranges.

Another configuration, that is often considered, is to include a source of “waste” or excess heat as supplement,
since this can increase the overall energy efficiency and reduce the required input energy. When used in
combination with High-temperature heat pumps, excess heat can be upgraded and therefore has the potential
to further reduce energy input drastically. However, the current technological maturity of these is not
comparable to resistive heating systems and are mainly available for pilot implementation in temperature
ranges above 200 °C (IEA HPT TCP 2023). In addition, high temperature heat pump implementations generally
require a source of high-quality waste heat to achieve high coefficients of performance (COP), which means
that the solutions quickly become very site-specific and not suitable for the generalized description presented
here.

Storage component
In Carnot batteries, energy is stored as heat. The Thermal Energy Storage (TES) component is a central part of
the Carnot battery and a main distinguisher from electro-chemical batteries. Three main categories of thermal
energy storages exist: sensible, latent and thermo-chemical.

In sensible TES, energy is stored by increasing the temperature of the storage in a single phase. Examples of
sensible TES are molten salt TES or solid rock TES. The charging cycle of this type of Carnot battery is done by
heating the material from the uncharged temperature to the charged temperature. Discharging is done by
removing heat from the storage material and thus lowering the temperature. Normal configurations are either
a single stratified tank where the charged (heated) material would tend to gather in the top, or two-tank
configurations. In a two-tank configuration, the energy is stored in the hot tank. When charging or discharging,

PAGE 75
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



the storage medium is heated and cooled while the heat transfer medium is being pumped or pushed from
one tank to the other.

Latent TES use the phase change, normally between solid and liquid forms, to store large amounts of energy.
Examples are ice storage (storing cold) and liquid-solid metal TES (storing heat). In latent TES the storage
material is forced to change phase (typically from solid to liquid) by heating the material during charging.
When discharging the material is allowed to solidify by removing heat from material. This allows for constant
discharging over a long period of time, since phase change will happen at constant temperature. A latent and
sensible combination can be achieved by further increasing the temperature of the phase change material
(PCM) beyond the melting point, where after the storage will behave as sensible heat storage.

Thermo-chemical TES uses reversible chemical reactions to achieve very high energy density. A reversible
chemical reaction that generates exothermic heat is used. An example could be the evaporation of water from
an inorganic salt while charging. The dried salt can be stored until the energy is required. When discharging,
steam is combined with the dried salt to produce a chemical reaction that produces heat that can be extracted
by conventional methods from the hydrated salt.

The three fundamental types of thermal energy storage are shown in Figure 4.

Sensible heat storage is the most common, cheapest and most technologically mature solution of the three
storage types, with large scale deployment of water-based solutions being used in buildings, district heating
and industrial heat application. The current deployment is mainly at temperatures up to 120 °C with sizes
ranging from small domestic sized units 50kWh to very large tank-based or pit storages with more than
1000MWh of heat stored. High temperature versions are in the early stage of commercial deployment with
large pilot plants being realized, typically for district heating applications or at industrial sites for process
heating or heat recovery. Latent heat storages that currently exist are being demonstrated at medium high
temperatures (<300 °C) and small-scale industrial sizes (<1MWh).

Figure 4 (a) Sensible (b) latent (c) thermochemical energy storage





Output conversion
In the type of Carnot battery described in this chapter, the stored heat is used to generate steam, which is
expanded in a turbine to drive a generator. This process is associated with conversion losses. Carnot batteries
with resistive heating, where the only purpose is electricity to electricity storage, are limited to round-trip-
efficiencies below 40% due to the efficiencies in the turbine-generator set (Danish Energy Agency 2024) .
These systems have, consequently, had limited success commercially due to the need for e.g., very favorable
input energy prices to secure a positive business case (Vandersickel 2023). Instead, commercial actors have
shifted their focus from pure power-to-power applications towards co-generation scenarios (e.g. using
refurbished co-generation plants) where the excess heat leaving the turbine is used for industrial applications
or district heating, allowing for total efficiencies approaching 90% (Danish Energy Agency 2024) Thermal
batteries) where the only output is heat, typically in the form of steam for industrial applications, have high
conversion efficiencies above 90%.

PAGE 76
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



Carnot batteries can theoretically reach very high efficiencies (Vandersickel 2023)], however this requires the
use of heat-pumping technology at the input stages. Such systems are sometimes referred to as pumped
thermal energy storage, analogues to pumped hydro storages. However, as mentioned above, the maturity of
such systems remains low with key technical complexity to overcome.

Energy efficiency and losses
As discussed above, the energy efficiency of a Carnot battery depends very much on the intended use case,
where extra conversion steps imply extra losses that multiply to reduce overall efficiency. In addition, for the
conversion steps, a range of technologies exists with varying maturity depending on the storage medium, the
size of the storage and energy form, and the quality requirements. A low-temperature storage with water at
atmospheric pressure would have very low thermal losses (<1% per day), however, low-temperature storage
has very poor efficiency in the conversion to electricity.

In contrast, a high temperature storage based on molten salts could be used to supply high-pressure steam
for a super-critical steam turbine with electrical conversion of >40%: However, the higher temperatures come
with higher losses to the environment. Thus, the round-trip efficiency (power-to-power) can end up at less than
30 %.

Regulation ability and other system services
The regulation ability of Carnot batteries is quite different from other batteries, especially since the battery is
capable of simultaneously charging and discharging due to the decoupling of input and output by the thermal
energy storage. In addition, Carnot batteries are commonly designed with non-symmetric charging and
discharging capacities with regulation capabilities that are very different.

Input: For resistive electric heating, that is commonly used as the main input conversion, the regulation
capabilities are very wide ranging and extremely fast in both up and down regulation (if up or down regulation
is possible). It is not uncommon for the capability of the input to be regulatable to the full rated capacity in
seconds or less by turning on or turning off the system (Danish Energy Agency 2024).

The main limitation is normally in the materials, so that much like a turbine / generator system, the input part
of the thermal energy storage would have to be in a hot or ready state in order to ramp up fast. Otherwise, the
rapid increase in temperature could cause damage to the materials by e.g. thermal expansion.

Output: For a turbine-based system the regulation capabilities are determined by the turbine generator
combination provided that the system is well designed with sufficient capacity of the heat exchanger and
steam generator. For Carnot batteries with turbine generators for power production, the full range of power
systems services are available, e.g., voltage and frequency stabilization, grid forming, inertia, as well as cold-
start capabilities. The regulation capabilities of typical steam-based Rankine cycle power plants can be found
in the technology catalogue for generation of electricity and district heating (Danish Energy Agency 2024).

Typical characteristics, capacities and storage periods
Carnot batteries are designed to fill a gap between the fast cycling of electro-chemical batteries and the slower
cycling of e.g., seasonal heat storage. A typical range for energy storage capacity is between 4-200 hours of
storage. The input and output can often be designed independently of each other and of the energy storage
capacity. Normal ranges for first-of-a-kind plants are in the range of 20-100 MW input/output power (thermal)
and 200-1000 MWh storage capacity, corresponding to 10-hour storage duration. This duration is useful for
e.g., day-to-night shifting of local renewable power generated by solar PV, which is prevalent in southern
Europe. It could also be used for night-to-day shifting of cheap electricity from the grid in power systems
dominated by wind power, for instance in the Nordic countries.

PAGE 77
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



Space requirement
Due to the high temperatures used in Carnot batteries, the energy densities are high, leading to low space
requirements for the energy storage part of a Carnot battery. In addition, since thermal energy storage tanks
can be built high instead of wide, compact designs are possible especially for very large energy storage
capacities.

Carnot batteries have auxiliary systems (e.g. cooling, oil) that increase the footprint. For applications where the
auxiliary systems are not required by other systems (e.g. in an urban setting for a district heating substation),
this will significantly increase the space requirements.

Advantages/disadvantages
The main advantage of Carnot batteries over other forms of storage is the size and expected price range that
makes them suitable for “medium to long duration” storage.

Energy storage systems are typically remunerated for the energy that flows through it. Therefore, to have an
acceptable economic outlook, there needs to be a reasonable balance between the number of cycles and the
costs of the battery system. For an expensive battery system, you would normally need to have a high cycle
count to achieve a low levelized cost of energy. The LCOS is the (discounted) cost per unit of discharged
electrical energy, i.e., more cycles equal lower LCOS. This has led to fast-acting stationary storage system,
being used mainly for ancillary service provision or short-term storage, e.g., peak-shifting. Accordingly, storage
durations for these battery types rarely exceed four hours of rated capacity, and the systems are designed to
be cycled daily or even more often.

In the other end of the spectrum, pit thermal energy storage (See 140 SEASONAL HEAT STORAGE) have found
successful application with as little as a single yearly cycle due to the several orders of magnitude reduction in
cost for the energy storage part.

By leveraging the cheap storage mediums available to thermal energy storage, e.g., rocks or molten salts,
Carnot batteries can offer much higher storage-to-input/output ratios at a cost where it is still possible to
achieve reasonable ROI and economic fundamentals. This is particularly true if used in situations where high
temperature heat or steam is required and valuable. If low grade excess heat can also be valorized, for
instance in the case where district heating networks can absorb the heat, an even better business case is
achievable.

The main disadvantage of Carnot batteries of the type described in this chapter is the poor power-to-power
efficiency. As discussed previously, the solutions are well suited to be implemented in situations where there is
a need for high-temperature heat or steam, e.g. for industrial applications or district heating. This effectively
limits the application of the technology to applications like industrial co-generation or combined heat-and-
power in population centers with district energy.

Environment
Carnot batteries based on molten salts, use common materials that are widely available, and where scaling of
production capabilities pose few risks and problems. Typical design use non-critical, non-toxic, non-flammable
materials for the storage of energy (Hyme (MOSS) 2024), (Heliac 2024) and can be constructed with little or no
use of so-called critical raw materials that are increasingly becoming a concern regarding self-sufficiency in a
European context (European Commision 2024).

Carnot batteries that use rocks as the storage medium can have an even lower environmental impact and can
be constructed using common and safe materials.

PAGE 78
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



Research and development perspectives
Carnot batteries have been the subject of dramatic increases in interest in the last decade, even if the
principles involved have been well understood for decades. The term Carnot batteries gained popularity in the
late 2010’s and recent years have seen great improvements in the technology overall.

From 2020 to 2023 in the IEA Energy Storage TCP (Technology Collaboration Program), Annex 36 focused on
bringing together researchers and practitioners within Carnot batteries. The annex focused on power-to-power
applications and was led by the German Aerospace Center (DLR), the annex gathered 15 countries and 47
institutions, and the aim of the work was to gather the state-of-the-art from around the world (Vandersickel
2023).

The work is currently continuing in Task 44 in the IEA Energy Storage TCP, which has adopted a wider scope,
including so-called “heat-integrated Carnot batteries”, to reflect that, as previously mentioned, a current
limitation for the technology is the poor round-trip efficiency for power-to-power storage.

Current focus of most research and development in molten salts-based systems focus on decreasing costs by
scaling towards either larger or more modularized designs, addressing potential corrosion issues that
compound with increasing temperatures, and thus reducing the need for expensive nickel-based alloys for e.g.
tanks and pipes.

In addition, work is ongoing on novel business models, e.g. storage-as-a-service or co-owned where problems
such as allocation of costs and benefits can be challenging.

Input conversion systems have so far focused on resistive heating elements, with commercial variants
available in relatively small sizes (<10 MW units) and at low voltage (<690V). To scale up, current research is
looking at increasing the voltage levels significantly to directly put plants in medium or high voltage grids
without the need for expensive downward transformations steps. (https://cetpartnership.eu/calls/funded-
project/locomosa, u.d.).

For output conversion, novel heat exchanger designs are being examined to fit to the thermophysical
properties of the molten salts. System designs using heat machines other than Rankine and Brayton cycle are
also being investigated – however such systems are generally much less mature, in fact most proposed
designs, while technically capable of being integrated with a power conversion unit (e.g., turbine generator
system), are moving towards heat as being the primary or even sole output.

The potential for Carnot batteries as back-up power generation
As mentioned previously, in the last few years the focus for the developers and researchers within Carnot
batteries have shifted towards heat integrated or heat-only solutions. This is analogous to the developments in
combined-heat-and-power plants in Denmark, where plants that were previously producing power as the
primary output and district heating was a bi-product, are increasingly being replaced by heat only units such as
biomass boilers, electric boilers or large heat pumps. Due to an increased number of low price or even
negatively priced hours in the electricity markets, the economics of co-generation plants have been under
pressure for several years. The situation is similar for a Carnot battery that could in theory be a 1-1
replacement of previous coal-, gas- or biomass-fired co-generation units. Techno-economic analysis of plant
economics shows that current prices in the electricity markets alone rarely allow for a reasonable payback
time for the capital-heavy plants. Thus, under current market conditions, some form of subsidy or capacity
payment is often required. However, since a Carnot battery would be able to cover both extremes of
increasingly volatile power prices, (energy arbitrage), this may change in the near future as the volatility is
expected to increase significantly with increased penetration of intermittent renewable energy. Expected
improvements in the technology, as described previously, are expected to lead to significant reductions in

PAGE 79
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



costs, so that future generations of Carnot batteries could play an important role in adequacy of supply and
power system stability.

The trend of developers going towards lower temperature ranges to bring down costs for customers in
industrial decarbonization scenarios, poses a risk that the technology will not be able to support the power
systems to the extent that would be socio-economically optimal. Analysis have shown that Carnot batteries
(Long Duration Energy Storage Council; McKinsey & Company 2021) can significantly decrease overall costs in
the energy transition (IEA 2021). As previously discussed, indications from the direction of both R&D and
announced projects are that current market conditions will not bring to market Carnot batteries with thermal
energy storages that utilize temperatures beyond 400 °C. The implication of this is that the possibility to
refurbish existing co-generation plants with thermal energy storages disappears, and thus the risk of
mothballed investments and loss of socio-economic value increases.

Examples of Market Standard Technology
Carnot batteries are currently in a phase where market standards are not well established. The most advanced
applications are large pilot and demonstration plants. In addition, due to the tendency of plants to be large
(since a key benefit of the technology is that the cost for storage of energy is much less per unit that
competing technologies), a high degree of customization is found, to tailor projects to fit well with existing
infrastructure.

On the other hand, Carnot batteries are essentially an exercise of system integration of the three major
components: Input conversion, thermal energy storage and output conversion. For this reason, commercial
development has made great strides in a relatively short period of time since the initial spike of interest in
Carnot batteries in the late 2010’s.

The major reason for this is that for some parts of the system, especially the input and output conversion,
mature technologies are commercially available, thus greatly reducing the technological risk.

Input conversion / charging capacity:

Resistive electric heating is available at large scale for temperature ranges approaching 600 °C for molten
salts and even higher for air-based systems. Typical module sizes are up to 5MW, with the possibility to
employ parallel operation of multiple heaters for increased charging capacity. As mentioned in the section on
R&D perspectives, current state-of-the-art uses low voltage (up to 690V).

Thermal energy storage

Thermal energy storage is widely used in industry today, most prominently is the use of tank thermal energy
storage in district energy systems. In a very broad definition, such systems could be considered a low
temperature version of a Carnot battery. In tank thermal energy storage, the thermal energy is stored in hot
water contained in steel tanks (See 141 Large-scale Hot Water Tanks). Pressurized versions allow for higher
temperatures and greater energy density but come at greater complexity and higher cost. To reduce costs,
mediums other than water are normally considered when higher temperatures are required, in order to stay at
atmospheric pressure within the storage system (Pressurized tanks and systems are much more costly to
design, build and operate due to the increased need for safety considerations and added strength).

Hot rocks and molten salt are two often used mediums.

Hot rocks have been considered due to their low material cost, thermal stability and high energy storage
capacity. With hot rocks, an additional heat transfer medium is required, typically either thermal oil or hot air

PAGE 80
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



(or steam). An example prototype from Danish firm Heliac is shown in Figure 5, and a similar concept from
Siemens Gamesa from a pilot plant in Hamburg in Figure 6.

Molten salt as a medium for thermal energy storage has been used in concentrated solar power plants for
decades (Fernández 2019) and the use of molten salt as a heat transfer medium in nuclear reactor designs is
also very well-established (LeBlanc 2010). However, historically the costs have been prohibitively high to find
use outside these niche applications. However, recent years have seen commercial companies develop stand-

Output conversion / discharge capacity

Output conversion is perhaps the most well-established part of Carnot batteries, where existing turbine
generators can be used for electricity generation and heat exchangers are commercially available for the
temperature ranges and most heat transfer mediums.

Prediction of performance and cost Uncertainty
In Figure 9, the technological maturity of the components of a Carnot battery is shown.

Carnot batteries are systems of components that are technologically mature (Dansk Center for energilagring
2023), except for the thermal energy storage component. The construction and operation of Carnot batteries

PAGE 84
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



as an integrated system is however still new. This leads to uncertainty both current and future costs. Since
Carnot batteries as a system are novel, they are expected to exhibit learning curves for their overall costs as
can be seen in Figure 9 (Long Duration Energy Storage Council, McKinsey & Co. 2022). Together this implies
that the overall cost of Carnot batteries will likely be significantly lower in coming years as the accumulated
production increases – this will be more pronounced for the energy storage component. For the energy
storage component, technological breakthroughs allowing for better handling of e.g. corrosion risks at high
temperatures are also expected to have a major impact on costs, since less and cheaper materials can be
used as corrosion mechanism and mitigation develop.

Increasing to higher temperatures in the storage and in applications also suggests lower maturity and
corresponding higher uncertainty and current costs. In a similar manner, for high temperature applications,
these are expected to have a more pronounced learning effect for the near term costs compared to lower
temperature versions (Long Duration Energy Storage Council, McKinsey & Co. 2022).

Figure 9 Technological development phase of Carnot batteries





For prediction of performance, the main uncertainty pertains to energy losses to the environment and lifetime.
In general, larger installations in big tanks reduce losses, while increasing temperatures will tend to increase
them. However, these kinds of losses are largely controlled by adding sufficient insulation to the tanks and
pipes. Depending on the expected storage period, this becomes an optimization parameter for a particular
installation and is thus rather site specific. Losses from pumping of heat transfer fluids are well known,
however for some of the more unusual salts, e.g. NaOH, systematic peer-reviewed results to confirm
simulation studies are still needed and thus the uncertainty here is relatively greater compared to the case of
air or thermal oils used in rock-based systems.

Uncertainty regarding costs is mainly on the thermal energy storages themselves, where initial cost estimates
still need to be verified when going to large scale commercial deployment, and thus the cost estimates for
these should be considered as subject to significant uncertainty. In contrast, most of the supporting (Danish

PAGE 85
143 Carnot Batteries and High-temperature Thermal Energy Storage



Energy Agency 2024) systems, as well as input and output conversion, are very mature where costs are known
to a high degree of certainty.

Quantitative description
There is a great deal of flexibility in the design and sizing of the three main components of a Carnot battery,
and these can be dimensioned independently to fit a specific site or use case. The input power can be scaled
so that charging times are suited for e.g., local renewable energy production from solar PV. In such cases the
charging capacity can be chosen to fill the storage in 6-8 hours while maintaining constant output of process
steam 24/7. In other cases, the process steam might only be required during daytime, but cheap electricity is
available during the night (in areas with high penetration of wind-power).

For this reason, for many of the technical inputs, ranges are given, and the capital costs have been split into
parts describing the input conversion given as cost per input power; the thermal storage given as cost per
storage capacity and output conversion given as cost per output power.

To interpret the data provided in the technology catalogue correctly, an example is provided: for an industrial
site, a continuous production of 15MW of process steam for a daily production of 8 hours, requiring a daily
output of 120MWh. With an input power of 10MW this can be accomplished in 12 hours of daily charging.
Depending on the kind of electricity available, the storage component can be sized accordingly, a storage
capacity of 240MWh would allow to use the 24 cheapest hours of electricity in a 48-hour period and could thus
provide significant cost savings. The same steam could be delivered by a very different type of Carnot battery
with 5 times the input power of 50MW allowing full charging for a 48-hour period in only 5 hours. Such a
Carnot battery would be more expensive to build but would be much better at capturing value in an electricity
market with higher volatility in electricity prices (Trevisan 2022).
